Report · page 21

Men's Southern League Divisions 3 & 6

With a mostly young team our Southern Leaguers maintained a high position in Division Three throughout the summer . We say mostly young but where would we be without veteran Mike Small who regularly wins just about every throw going, setting veteran personal and club bests into the bargain. Things got a little harder as the season progressed and we lost sprinter Marlon Dickson, vaulter Jamie Webb and 400 flat and hurdles man Jon Squirrell to the British League team but to be honest, this just gave some of the younger men a chance to show their mettle - and they grasped the chance.

Going down to Bracknell for the final match we needed 1st or 2nd team spot to clinch promotion. It wasn't made easier when Team Manager Bob Bridges went sick on the day. We abandoned our Division Six team entirely at this point as Don Anderson stepped in as Team Boss. Approaching the relays our boys felt they had done enough for the day in the hot sun and incredibly, with a match win and promotion beckoning, it looked as if we would fail to turn out a 4x400 squad. A quick chase round by Pat Mead rounded up a somewhat less than enthusiastic team who donned their spikes while Kenny Pereira occupied our lane and held up the event - much to the annoyance of the officials. They might not have wanted to do it but our lads ended up with a fine win with Richard David running a cracking 48.2 for the last leg. The match was ours. Promotion was secured and a sick Bob was 'phoned up and given the news.

As we have said, we abandoned the Division Six team at the end. It had been a great struggle to fill out a team for much of the time - although we do have quite a few vets and youngsters who enjoy competing for our third team. The penalty for failing to fill out 50% of the events is an automatic drop of two divisions so it's Division Eight in '97 for the old boys. There is an advantage here, however. It's only one per event in Division Eight so things should be a little easier to organise here while our second team launches an attack on Division Two with the aim of going back to the first division in '98.

Report · page 22

Women's Southern League Div. 3

After a win at home in match 1 hopes were high that we could maintain our effort and at last gain promotion from Division Three. Not to be. After several seasons of being short of under 15s, at Hayes and Sutcliffe Park we suddenly found ourselves scratching around for seniors to fill out the team and paid the penalty with two 4th places.

It was a different story for match 4 at Aldershot. Sprinter Sabrina Scott turned out while in the 3,000 metres we were treated to a dominating run from Samantha Northey as she ran away from the combined Division One and Six fields to win by a street. Success wasn't confined to the seniors either as Debbie Hearn and Rebecca Lester both came from behind to outkick the rest of the runners in their 800m races. In the end four points covered the first three teams and it was 2nd for the "Belles".

Two matches to go and we were slowly dragging ourselves back up the league table. At the Royal Navy track at Portsmouth our throwers were in good form with 16 year-old Michelle Stock winning the 'A' discus and 'B' shot, and Angela Patten and Gail Richardson also scoring good points. Andrea Abrams took charge of the long jump and Natalie Law and Sabrina Scott were unbeaten in both sprints and relays. Try as they could though, the under 15s were unable to come up with any wins this time, and we were 3rd team on the day.

Holidays took their toll for the final match at home and wins were few and far between. Two victories that did stand out were Sam Northey's Under 17 3000m Club Record followed by her 1500m performance. Even spectating British League 800m/1500m man Dave Pamah was impressed!

And so we stay where we are for another year. Jacqui Smiter has survived her first year as Joint Team Manager and we now set our sights on 1997. Can we get up to Division Two this time?

Report · page 24

Men's National Junior League - Southern Area Division 2

If ever there was a team going places then this is the one. Many of our Junior Men's team have been with us since they were 13 or 14 and now they are doing battle as under 20s, a good percentage of them with another season to go in the same age group. With promotion in two successive years we go into 1997 in Division One with a real chance to make a name for ourselves. Each of the matches, with one notable exception, was won by an avalanche of points. Not surprising really when you consider that some of these guys handle themselves with distinction in British League matches, so so lining up against Division Two Juniors enabled them to make mincemeat of the opposition.

At each meeting a "Man of the Match" award is made and on occasions a choice had to be made from five or six Belgravians who had turned in eye-opening performances; 10.8 hundreds, 21.9 200s, 48 second 400s, 1:51 800s, 15 metre triple jumps, low 14 sprint hurdle runs, the list goes on and on.

One man who didn't get a man of the match award was Marlon Dickson but for most supporters he was the "Man of the Season" as he scorched to win after win. Continuing his success from match 1 he went on to score doubles in each of the remaining meets with 10.9/22.0, 10.8/21.7 and 10.9/21.9 not to mention having a large part to play in the relay teams.

Our ancient sprint relay record went in match 1 and at Tooting it was the turn of the 4x400m to go the same way as Michael Parper and Richard David both went sub-50 for their individual splits. Michael had already impressed with his 48.9 individual win while Steve Scott's 14.3 sprint hurdles victory took him to the top of the national rankings for the year. But it was Phillip Idowu's performance that was rated most highly as he leapt out to 15 metres for the second week in a row.

Woodford was the one exception teamwise. A poor turnout left gaping holes in our team which in turn led to only 3rd team place but there was plenty to savour in the way of record performances. Tom Lerwill made an appearance before setting off for Sydney and the World Junior Championships. First up he cruised to a relaxed 800m win which at 1:51.5 was just a tenth shy of the National League Record. Later his 48.2 400m equalled the league best but it was the long relay that was most eagerly anticipated as we had gathered together James Hilston, Richard David and Michael Parper to join with Tom in a crack at the 4x400m record.

Of course some of these lads are no mean sprinters over the 'hundred' and first they wanted to hand out some stick in the short relay. Steve Scott blasted leg one and overtook each man outside him before handing on to James who ... popped his hamstring as he took the baton! For an instant his instinct was to run on ... but he fell ... while the last two runners stood and waited for a baton that never came.

Now what? With one of our key men clutching an ice pack to his thigh in the St. John's hut we were a man short ... or were we. We had reckoned without the courage of Marlon Dickson. Having already recorded his normal double he was now the natural choice to stand in for James. He wanted to do it, but never having run a 400 in anger before you can imagine his feelings as he was talked into running lap one. With dry mouth went to his mark and when the starter's pistol sent the runners away we knew that record was on again as Marlon kept his speed in check but flowed to a marvellous 49.2. What a debut - and he must have two to three seconds more under the bonnet! With an already enormous lead Richard David and Michael Parper stretched it still further and when Tom Lerwill finished with 47.6 for his stint that National Record had been blown away by SIX WHOLE SECONDS!

With a virtually full team for the final meeting at Battersea it was win after win throughout the afternoon. In circumstances like this it is difficult to pick out special performances for mention - but here's two. Peter McDonald took miles out of his best 3000 metre time as he ran 9:24.3 (and still had the 'chase to come) and Joe Panton, always good for a low 52 400m, tried his hand at the two lap race and timed it to perfection as he turned on his speed in the finish straight to record a winning first ever attempt at the event.

Well done boys. We await the '97 season with bated breath.

Report · page 25

Rosenheim League

Winning streak stretches to ten!

Coming out of match 3 at Wimbledon Park with a third straight win and a five point buffer over the next best team, thoughts began to centre on the possibility of our Rosenheim Raiders taking the series right through the summer without loss. Could it be done? The later matches are the more difficult ones though and great persuasion has to be used to get our team to the unpopular venues of Croydon and Sutton.

Wimbledon saw some super sprinting and Mike Edwards tucked away another ground record when he cleared 4.85 for a n/s pole vault. It was also good to see that a small nucleus of our Women's Team were beginning to keep the lads company and tackle the non-scoring events.

On to Croydon then. It might not be our most favourite venue - but then the other clubs didn't seem to like it much either and once again our team took their match tally over 60 points for win number four. Ben Williams collected a fine sprint double and Mike Small, as usual, kept things ticking over in the throws. For Jonathon Squirrell it was a chance to show what he can do in his favourite event - the 400m hurdles and he stormed round for a clear win.

At Sutton Arena it was Richard David's turn to notch up a sprint double while Gavin Mason returned to the team to collect top points in the 400m. Gerry Adams had a crack at an unfamilar event when he lined up for the 2000m 'chase - not sure that he really enjoyed himself but it was more points in the bag as we cruised on to team win number five.

So ... to the final Eastern Division match at Tooting. Had any Club ever won every match in a season? Herne Hill's Kevin Kelly assured us that it had been done before - so we needn't worry ourselves on that score! The match got under way and James Hilston had a remarkable run in the 100m. He was left for dead when it appeared there had been a false start and he only belatedly began running after standing up and then realising that there would be no recall. How he got up to 2nd we'll never know.

Only four individual wins this time but how nice to get a Belgravian home first in the 3000 metres as Roger Alsop did everything right during his 7½ lap stint. You'd have thought that 17 year-old Marlon Dickson's 21.6 200m had to be the event of the evening but something special was being planned by our Junior Men. James Hilston, Richard David and Michael Parper teamed up with Marlon to turn on one of the best 4x200m relays ever seen. Averaging 21.97 per man they ran clean away from the opposition and just kept on going. Remember they are all Juniors but their final time of 1:27.9 was just a watch's tick over the league record and will surely be the fastest club time of the year. It was a Junior Club Record by over three seconds.

And so we'd done it. Six out of six. But the real battle was to come. All summer long Stan Allen had been saying that Herne Hill's aim was just to reach the final and one had the feeling that now they'd got there they were gearing themselves to teach the Bels a thing or two. Essentially our team had been the usual mix of talented Under 20s, a couple of veterans and a couple of seniors on the verge of breaking out of the Southern League and into the BAL team. We went into the final with the same mix and found some very unfamiliar faces drafted into the opposing teams.

Again Richard David took the 100m but it was the 400m where he really impressed. Running in the non-scoring race he blitzed to 48.7. Dave Pamah provided more front running in the two lap event as he totally ignored the field and made it hot and fast all the way - let the others hang on if they could.

We had struggled to find a high jumper for the meet but James Hilston, carrying a ham-string injury, had no thought of us losing any points. The injury was not to his take-off leg and he felt he'd be able to do himself justice. Justice? He equalled the league record with a mighty 2 metre leap!

There was drama too in the triple jump where Mat George was having a disastrous evening. It was neck and neck with Herne Hill so every single point counted - and Mat, expected to be fairly well up there, had a series that read: foul, foul, foul, rubbish, foul. Some quiet encouragement from the Team Manager and wallop - 13.28 and second place.

Our big man Mike Small made his way from throw to throw, picking up two second places but it was all very, very tight. As Division Winners we had taken a 4 point advantage into the final and as it turned out, we eventually won by just that margin. Herne Hill had matched us for every point on the night and had even scored as many wins as we had.

But the Rosenheim Plate was ours for another year. Seven wins out of seven in 1996 and ten in a row all told. There's no way the other local clubs are going to take that lying down in '97!

Feature · page 26

Don't be surprised when Michael Johnson runs under 43 seconds

Bob Bridges

To start off with it might be an idea to go to the spare room, root through that pile of athletics magazines lying in the corner, and dig out Athletics Weekly for August 21st 1996. Turn to pages 32 and 33 to read Play The Percentages Game by 400 metre coach Mike Smith - and now read on. ... The one thing missing from the article is how fast you should run the first 200m of a 400m race. Now everyone is different so I have to generalise to a certain extent but I believe that the first 200m should be run one second outside of maximum - and this is what I tell my athletes to aim for. The differential between first and second 200s should be 1.5 seconds. Both of these are conservative figures.

To test the theory: The 1995 season saw James Hilston run a 200m best of 22.4 secs. (He was injured through much of 1996 so we'll take no notice of more recent times.) Therefore, his first 200m in a one-lap race should be 23.4 and using the remainder of the above formula - 23.4 x 2 + 1.5 = 48.3. His best time that year was actually 48.35. We are talking here about a relatively novice level when compared to international athletes so let's move on and test the theory with some of Britain's finest in 1996: Roger Black. Best 1996 200m 20.56. Therefore 21.56 x 2 + 1.5 = 44.62 (actual best 44.34). Mark Richardson. Best 1996 200m 20.6. 21.6 x 2 + 1.5 = 44.70 (actual best 44.66). Jamie Baulch. Best 1996 200m 21.97. 21.97 x 2 + 1.5 = 45.46 (actual best 44.57).

As you can see, the times of 1 sec. and 1.5 secs. are quite conservative at top level but it is not easy to judge as most of the top 400 metre runners do not run that many quality 200 metre races and it is difficult to know what their potential 200m best really is. Except one, and that's Michael Johnson.

Apply the above formula to Michael's times. His 1996 200m best of 19.32 gives us 20.32 x 2 + 1.5 = 42.14 secs. Or even using his old 200m World Record figures of 19.66 - 42.82.

What I conclude from this is that either Michael Johnson is scared of 400s or he needs a kick up the backside. He had a very low differential of only 0.35 secs in last year's World Champs 400m final (21.65 and 22.00) and I believe that he ran the first half too slow. If he had run one second faster, still only 20.65, and kept his same second half then a time of 42.65 was feasible. I know it would be difficult to achieve this at a major championships with all the preliminary rounds but the above figures are conservative and in a one-off at a Grand Prix it should be possible. So don't be surprised when Michael Johnson runs under 43 seconds ... or maybe even under 42 !

Bob Bridges

Bob BridgesMike SmithJames HilstonRoger BlackMark RichardsonJamie BaulchMichael Johnson

Report · page 28

McDonalds Young Athletes League Girls Division 2 South

It has to be said that our attempt to move into the Girls' McDonalds Young Athletes League in 1996 was not successful. Probably the main problem is that we just do not have enough young female members - and those that we do have are not too keen on turning out in the YA League on Sunday when the odds are that they have competed in the Southern Women's League on the Saturday. The fact that the SWL includes events for younger age groups is obviously faced by other clubs as well as ourselves but without the numbers the task is impossible. So, increasing our female membership has to be addressed first - or does it ? We don't want to overload those that are running our women's teams - so maybe we need more people to take responsibility for organisation - and where do they come from? In the meantime we have withdrawn from the league for now and will concentrate on Women's Southern and Thameside Leagues.

Report · page 29

Veterans' Track & Field Mid-London Division

Double win for the "Serps"

The competition is always tight between Serpentine, Metropolitan Police and Belgrave as far as the men's matches go. We are so strong in the throws and pick up points all over the place in over 50 events thanks to Captain Snowy. Just to level up the race for points, however, we always seem give ourselves the handicap of going into each match with an almost total lack of middle-distance runners. We've got enough of them in the club but they just don't seem interested in track competition. Ian Vaughan-Arbuckle was a welcome addition to our over 50 runners and Rex Bale filled in when he could. Nice too, to have Derek Clack back in the team while the ever eager Mick Graham will do anything to get on the score sheet - but that was it as far as 800m, 1500m and 3000m and steeplechase were concerned - for both age groups. As a result, as South of England Champions we failed to even make the main final this year and just scraped into the auxiliary final as runners up in our division.

By the time of the auxiliary final we were able to welcome some MD runners into the side but by then the sprinters and jumpers were suffering from a variety of pulls and twinges and as the match slowly slipped away from us we pulled Snowy out of his 'kamikaze' decathlon-in-one-day points quest and put things on ice until 1997.

There is absolutley no stopping Serpentine Runners in the Women's matches and as we do not now have anything like a full team it's just a question of doing the events that take one's fancy and enjoying the outing. Amazingly this resulted in a tight competition where we just lost out to the Mets. at the end of the season by one league point - and then the 'Girls in Blue' announced that they were pulling out of the auxiliary final and offered us their place.

With the same tactics in the final the Belles cruised into 5th spot - one place better than the men - and rounded off their season with a 4x100m run for the pure joy of just being out there and doing it. With two of the team over 50, another knocking on the door and one over 60, there was no way they were going to win but the sight of our W60 throws specialist Barbara Dunsford giving it some down the finishing straight was one to treasure.

Photograph · page 29

Boys of Summer

Centre page photo captions - starting top left and working clockwise.

1.At 16 James Tuson's a bit of an all-rounder. Last season, in his first year as an U17M he high jumped 1.80, long jumped 5.75, tripled 13.10 and cropped up regularly in sprints and relays. James is our U17M T&F Captain for 1997. 2.Clearly our most prolific points scorer in British League matches, Discus thrower Kevin Brown's 1996 best of 58.66 ranked him 3rd in the country. His all-time best is 59.50 from '95. He's not averse to heaving the shot either and pictured overleaf is about to take 2nd place in BAL match 3 'B' event at Alexander Stadium. 3.Lee Newman specialises in Shot and uses the spiral technique. His 1996 best of 18.85 was a pb that placed him 4th in the country but in indoor competition this winter he has already shown signs that his best is due for revision yet again. 4.At Kingsmeadow for BAL match 4 Paul Evans and Gary Staines toy with the opposition in the 5,000 metres before running in together for 'A' and 'B' wins, separated by the electrical timing by 1/100th! 5.One time Gladiators contestant Chris Rawlinson set personal records of 14.52 and 50.36 for the two hurdle races in '96 - as well as gaining an international vest. He's no mean decathlete either with a score of 6,291 dating from 1995. 6.Ian Tullett catapaults himself to 5.20 at Copthall. What great service our pole vaulters have given us over the years. 7.Yet another Belgrave thrower to set his best ever performance during 1996. Leith Marar reached out a further 20 cm to 55.58 to rank 8th in the country for the Discus. Leith was second only to Kevin Brown when it came to points scoring in BAL matches. 8.Mike Edwards vaulted to 5.40 to win at Copthall in BAL match 2 but his season's best of 5.45 came at Battersea in a Rosenheim event. 9.Matt Yates scored some great double wins over 800 and 1,500 metres. Pictured at Copthall where he had already taken the 'A' 800 event, he controls the 'B' 1,500m before sprinting away from the field.

Photo credits: Paul Beaumont - 5 and 7. Alan Mead - 1, 2 and 3 Robin Nicholls - 6, 8 and 9. Ray O'Donoghue - 4.

Report · page 32

McDonalds Young Athletes League Boys Southern Premier Division: Bels make it to the National Finals

With the fortunes of our younger athletes reported in detail in our Youth In Action magazine we will more or less restrict ourselves here to publication of the results to keep the historical information contained within the pages of The Belgravian intact. However, we must report that 1996 saw Belgrave's young male athletes attain their most impressive achievements yet. True, we have been in the Southern Premier Division before, but it was only a brief showing in 1994 before dropping back down again. This time it was different. With three team wins and two second places not only did we finish up with a top three placing but we made it to the National finals at Alexander Stadium, Birmingham, something that no other club has managed to do when only just breaking into the Division. The standard at this level is ferocious in an arena where the very cream of the nation's Young Athletes do battle for their clubs.

It's pretty ferocious off the field of competition as well as clubs vie for every possible point and rival teams invoke the rarely used rule of deducting points where clubs do not provide enough officials. Managing the team involves the nightmare task of bringing together maybe sixty boys, transporting them across the country and then making sure that they compete in club kit, adhere exactly to the competition rules and turn up at the start of their event on time. And as for the final, involving an overnight stay in a hotel in the Midlands ... Surfice it to say that the Belgrave Team Management pairing of Leo Coy and John Powell was equal to every crisis. Not only must we congratulate our athletes and their Captains but we must underline what an amazing achievement it has been for these two men.

But things move on. As reported elsewhere, Leo is moving to Ireland and will no longer be seen in his familiar role as Team Manager. John Powell is also moving on, but in his case to manage our Under 20 Men's team as they too shoot for top team honours. Taking their places will be two new managers who will carry Belgrave's hopes for even more success: Laurence Rogers and Barbara George. We wish them luck.

Sean Eaton ran to 3rd and 4th places in the Under 13 National Rankings for 100 and 200 metres with 12.3 and 26.0, times set when he was still eleven years of age. With another summer in the age group we can expect fireworks from this boy in 1997- but then with an Aunt like GB international Lorna Booth would you expect otherwise.

Leo CoyJohn PowellLaurence RogersBarbara GeorgeSean EatonLorna BoothAlan Mead

Report · page 34

In the Northumberland All-Terrain Marathon at the beginning of August Chris Sweeney found hinself running on beach, woodland and track to take part in this made for TV all-terrain event.

Unfortunately Chris had to come out with a hamstring injury before its conclusion. He's now slowly getting back to fitness and its a shame that such a fine runner picks up so many niggles. 4m Road Holy Island 14, 19:25. 4m Hill Ingram 13, 23:30. 1m Plessey Woods 7, 4:41. 2m Track Hexham 9, 9:08. 3m Beach Embleton Bay 16, 15:25. 3m Harbour Seaton Sluice 13, 15:23.

In the Amsterdam to Zaandam Dam to Dam 10m. Josephat Machuka averaged 4:31.8 per mile to set a World Best 45:18.world best. Paul Evans with 46:36 was the only man in the first 12 not an African!

After the opening rounds of the IAAF World Cross Challenge Paul Evans found himself lying 26th in the international standings. A 17th place at Durham over 9.3k in 29:54 was followed a week later with 15th in Belfast over 8k in 24:15. Matt Yates looking more confident under new coach Bruce Longden challenged for the lead in the middle distance men's 2k and ended up placing 3rd to John Mayock, his 6:17 was just two seconds down.

Chris SweeneyJosephat MachukaPaul EvansMatt YatesBruce LongdenJohn Mayock

Report · page 34

Hilary keeps the flag flying on the roads

Despite attending degree course lectures three nights a week at London University together with the associated coursework and in addition to her full time job at the Public Record Office, Hilary Saw has kept the Belgrave colours flying on the roads, finishing as second W45 in the season-long Surrey Road League. Hilary's results were as follows: Herne Hill 10k & Surrey Champs. 16th female 49:10; Epsom All-Sorts 10k 51st, 51:24; Epsom Fun Day 5m 32nd (2ndW45) 39:43; Coombe Hill 5m 53rd (2nd W45) 39:17; Tadworth 5m 161st, 40:48; Dorking 10m 50th female 86:47; Dysart Dash 10k 298th, 53:29; Elmbridge 10k 276th (8th W45) 50:44; Belgrave & Surrey 5k 12th female 23:32; Vets AC 5.2m 6th female (1st W45) 40:38.

Hilary Saw

Records · page 35

Men's Track & Field Rankings 1996

Hazel Mead

Here they are again - as deep as ever. How far up (or down!) can you go in 1997. As usual, wind readings are shown where known and the numbers before the athlete's name indicate World/British ranking for their age group - again where known. In the odd case where a Junior (U20M) athlete is ranked nationally among Seniors then their senior ranking is additionally shown in brackets. Wind assisted marks are shown at the end of each event except where we do not know of a non-wind assisted performance in which case they have been included in the main list to preserve the 'pecking order'. Notification of any errors or additions should be sent to Belgrave Rankings, [address withheld].

Men's Track & Field Rankings compiled by Hazel Mead

Eighteen year old one-lap racer Michael Parper came back to the Belgrave Rankings with a vengeance, clocking 48.54 at Bedford in the AAA U20 Champs. One of four Belgravians ranked in the Junior UK Top 20 for the 400m event, Michael has been elected our Under 20 Men's Captain for 1997.

Steve Scott - number one U20 in the country over the 99cm 110m hurdles with two more years in the age group!

Every year another improvement. In 1996 Jamie Webb raised his best 'vault another 15cm to 4.40m and broke into the British League team for the first time.

Left: Kevin Brown finished the season as Belgrave's most prolific points scorer in British Athletic League matches. His 80 points put him 10 points clear of fellow thrower Lee Newman.

Apart from his exploits in individual 800m events, Tom Lerwill had a part to play in two of our 4x400m records of 1996. In Istanbul he joined Ridgeon, Rawlinson and Nolan for a 3:09.30 clocking and pictured above, he anchored our under 20 squad to a 3:17.1 National Junior League record with a 47.6 effort.

Report · page 41

Best ever year for relays

In the European Clubs Championship a new 4x400 metres Club Record was set by Messrs. Lerwill, Ridgeon, Rawlinson and Nolan, and there's a couple of names there who went on to set superb individual performances as the season progressed. But talking of relays, Juniors Scott, Dickson, Johnson, Hilston, Parper and Lerwill wiped up the previous 4x100, 4x200 and 4x400 marks. With some of them only just turned 17 their Under 20 Men's 4x200 mark, set during a Rosenheim League match, averaged 21.9 per man and the time has stood up as the fastest Club time in the UK this year - in Senior competition, let alone Junior. Their 4x400 removed 6 whole seconds from the National Junior League Record and was close to the British Junior Best Ever. Even with Lerwill exiting the age group in 1997 that mark is still in danger.

LerwillRidgeonRawlinsonNolanScottDicksonJohnsonHilstonParper

Report · page 48

Hewitt-Jones Road Relay, Walton, 14 Sep 96

First fixture of the 'winter' season and a minibus laden to the gunnels with a cheerful band of runners. What more can a Team Manager want.

The old Walton Relay has been suffering from poor entries in recent years. Times change and the 'road' course now takes in five or six hundred metres of woodland to avoid some of the traffic and miss the pot-holes in the layby no longer maintained by the council. But this year entries were up again and to avoid a mass of runners surging out onto the road at the start, the ladies and veterans were sent on their way some five minutes after the senior men.

Fresh from a good track season Dave Pamah gave the Bels a flying start. Boxhill were away through Haywood but Dave's powerful finish, delayed until the pack were out of the woods, carried the claret and gold to 2nd and his 15:59 stood up as our fastest of the day. Roger Alsop disposed of the 'Boxhill Billies' but Jackson of Aldershot was not to be denied as he set the day's fastest and gave his club a lead they were not to relinquish.

On a flying visit from Dublin, Derek Kelleher took charge on stage three and with twin Dave urging him on, set off like a champion. Sadly he had a bad day, blowing up at the foot of the hill and finding himself unable to respond as our rivals pounced. He was so dejected afterwards. Never mind old chap, it can happen to the best.

Herne Hill were now in 2nd while we were 5th - but we had a strong second half up our sleeves. Dave Beaver took us forward again with yet another 'blinding' run and had our next man Said Chettati racing flat out to get to the take-over zone ahead of him. It might have looked hilarious but the Team Manager was having kittens. Not the best way to start his stage but Said held on to his position, resisting the Hare & Hounds man breathing down his neck.

No problems on the last lap as Gerry carried the Bels away from any danger of finishing out of the frame, gaining half a minute on AFD and a whole minute on Boxhill.

It was good to get a full 'B' squad out as well as a veterans' team, and youngsters Steve Beaver and Kevin Quinn should be delighted with their efforts.

23 teams started, 20 finished.

Fastest: J.Jackson (Aldershot F&D) 15:16.

17 teams finished.

Fastest: M.Waine (Woking) 16:41.

Report · page 48

Great North Run, Newcastle 15 Sep 96: Tyneside Triumph

It was Paul Evans' sixth trip to the North-East for the Great North Run Half-Marathon and it was reckoned by many to be his best chance yet of pulling off a victory with the field not quite so packed with Kenyans as in recent times. Even when the organisers enrolled three time winner Benson Masya at the eleventh hour the Belgrave Harrier could still claim to be at least equal favourite for the Kenyan had not shown his customary form in recent outings. For Gary Staines it was his second attempt at this classic, the race forming part of his preparations for the Chicago Marathon and as he gets more and more comfortable with the longer road races he moves ever closer to Paul's shoulder.

The race was an absolute treat for Belgrave supporters glued to the television as a small but elite group broke away from the masses and included Masya, the Spaniard Antonio Serrano, Basildon's Eamonn Martin and Rob Denmark and the Belgrave trio of Evans, Staines and Kassa Tadesse. With Jim Estall out of TV camera range we could not see our fourth placer but knew that our "oh-so-reliable" super-vet was sure to be forward enough to clinch the team race should things continue in this vein.

Denmark was first to fall away with bad blisters and at 10k Serrano accelerated to shake the rest up but it was at the 10 mile point that the decisive break came as Benson Masya decided that the others were too close for comfort. He didn't pull far away and for the rest of the race, particularly on the downhill run in to the coast it seemed that Paul might just get back to him but it was not to be.

Gary easily got the better of his Chicago rival Martin for 4th and Kassa managed a tremendous sprint to beat Morpeth's Hudspith across the line for 7th. Great to see Kassa running well again. Sure enough, a steady run from Jim had him home in 60th spot and when the times were added up we had the beating of Morpeth and Bingley by around seven minutes.

As after the London Marathon there was some comment on the fact that our men wore two designs of vest - the fully professional Evans and Staines clad in their Nike kit (as per their contracts) with the other two wearing traditional claret and gold. But as a letter from Woodford Green's Terry McCarthy later stated in AW: ". what a can of worms this rule is .. " The letter was headed "Forget the vests, Belgrave boys were best." We can only agree.

Feature · page 48

After 15 months of running with the Bels, Dave Beaver seems more surprised than anyone with his success. Last season in his first race for us he went into our scoring ten in the Surrey League ... and at Lloyd Park this winter in race three of the series he became our first man home. A member of our winning Surrey CC Team he very nearly earned himself an Inter-Counties run. All this through keeping son Stephen company and advising him on his training runs. But then talent will out. You've got to remember that this man,at the age of 15 in 1975, set what is probably still a UK Age Best for 5,000m of 14:54.6! And of course, in 1978 he placed 2nd to Steve Binns in the National Junior CC Champs at Luton.

Past

Left: It's November 26th 1977 at Parliament Hill and showing no fear, eighteen year-old Dave Beaver (253), racing in the classic Polytechnic Harriers singlet, locks horns with "Bootsie" Dave Bedford (284) in the London Cross Country Championships. Bedford won with Dave 4th and Bob Treadwell (329 in the photo) 2nd.

Present

Still blowing hard after the traditional Christmas Day work-out - running in the Lauriston Paarlauf.

Dave is regularly at Belgrave Hall on Sunday mornings, ready to run at 10:30am, and will be delighted to pass on his experience to any youngsters between the ages of about 15 to 20 who want to run with him and Stephen over distances from five to eight miles.

Future

Right: It can't always be easy having a father who's done it all himself but 15 year-old Steve Beaver enjoys his running and proudly wears his Surrey vest in an Inter-Counties meet at Southampton.

Far right: Showing all the style of a thoroughbred, Steve heads a mixed bunch during an Open Meeting middle-distance race at Tooting.

Report · page 49

Reebok South of England Road Relays, Rushmoor Arena, Aldershot, 28 Sep 96: Good ol' boys see us through

On the journey down to Aldershot we were reliably informed that this was the first time we had tackled the event as a senior team since 1991. Our informant, Roger Alsop, went on to let us know that he was the only survivor from that team five years ago when he turned in the slowest time for the Belgrave outfit. Now he vowed to be the fastest.

The intention was that we should aim for a place in the top 20 and thus qualify for a place in the National 6-Stage when we might well be able to pull in most of our "top-guns" but as race day neared, of our 36 entered names only six were available on the day and it was going to be touch and go. Poor Charlie Dickinson, who had not intended even running in the veterans event and hadn't even got his racing shoes with him, found himself dragged into the senior squad within minutes of the race starting.

With 110 teams toeing the start line Gerry took his turn as lead-off man and after his two circuits of 2,961 metres (5,922m in all) brought us home a respectable 17th. The melee of the second leg saw wholesale changes in the team positions and Said slipped back six places although he was pleased with his time in relation to Cap'n Gerry's.

True to his word Roger Alsop put up the fastest Belgrave time on leg three and underlined yet again that he is entering the 96/97 season at a totally new level of fitness. Junior Galley was next up, suffering from lack of sleep after the arrival of a new baby Galley a few days earlier, and when a plucky Chas. Dickinson completed his stint we hovered just on the 20 mark.

There was excitement all round on the last leg. Blackheath had led from stage 3 and must have thought they had the race in the bag but Chelmsford's Keith Cullen believed otherwise and flew round for the 3rd best clocking of all time, taking his Essex team to victory.

For the Bels. a tanned Jim Estall gobbled up four of the teams ahead and all things considered we had to be pleased with our 16th place. Mission accomplished! Jim's effort was superb, the only other veterans to run faster times being Invicta's Mike Gratton (18:51) and Cambridge Harrier's Alan Camp (18:56), while Julian Goater could only offer a 19:08.

An unexpected third place for the understrength Belgrave M60 team made their journey to Rushmoor Arena worthwhile. Missing both Laurie O'Hara, who has been off colour all summer, and new 60's star Tony Verdie who couldn't turn out, our four were delighted to pinch their bronze on the last leg when Woodford Green ran out of runners.

Most pleasing was the return of Mike Newton who has been troubled by a succession of illness problems over the last couple of years. Mike ran a blinding 24:57 just fractionally slower than the fine first leg run by Pat Newell which had put Belgrave in 6th place. Pat hadn't long returned from walking the Pennine Way, so must have found the hills at Aldershot a piece of cake!

A rather unfit Charlie (Linford) Walker ran a steady leg in spite of having to return to touch Mike after a false start. Carl Cross too ran steadily on the last stage and it was here that Belgrave's strength in depth showed as we eased into the medals above teams with just one or two faster runners.

It was good to see the M50's team out again with Richard Pearson, now elevated to that age group, running very strongly.

110 teams started, 88 finished.

Fastest: 1, K.Cullen (Chelmsford) 17:09; 2, A.Bristow (Brighton & Hove) 17:35; 3, D.Laughlin (Chelmsford) 17:36.

32 teams finished.

Fastest: 1, M.Rouse (Invicta EK) 20:06; 2, G.Jones (Southampton City) 20:20; 3, M.Rutland (Oxford City) 20:55.

8 teams finished.

Fastest: 1, R.Davidson (Cambridge H) 21:22; 2, R.Higgs (Barnet) 22:31; 3, P.House (Woodford Gn) 22:54.

Report · page 49

Great Caledonian Run 10k, Edinburgh, 29 Sep 96

Only Britons, including Gary Staines, had previously triumphed in the Caledonian run but with the course changed to take in the rugged slopes of Arthur's Seat, it was the Kenyans who overcame the additional adversity with Kelong and Nziku racing it out between them to decide the winner. Staines and Longwood's Pearson seemed to be tucked in nicely but the descent made them look decidedly uncomfortable and as the field ran onto the Royal Mile the Africans were away.

Report · page 49

Great South Run 10m, Portsmouth, 6 Oct 96

Looking totally at home back on the South Coast, Gary Staines put the finishing touches to his Chicago Marathon preparation with a scintillating victory against classy opposition. It was his third win in the event and what a trio of times - 46:11, 47:00 and 46:57!

Commonweath Games 10,000m Champion Lameck Aguta joined Gary and and Skyrac's Whitehead as Ian Cornford of Shaftesbury forced the early pace. Only Degefu from Ethiopia managed to bridge the gap from the main pack but before long it was the Belgravian taking the initiative. As the others dropped off one by one it was left to Aguta and Staines to chop and change in front while the Ethiopian tucked in behind. As the Kenyan went ahead for the final stages now it was Gary tucking in behind as the windiest stretch of the race was reached. Just a few hundred metres to go and it was was a delighted Staines who had the race totally under control as he came home by a one second margin.

Back down the field young Kevin Quinn did not impress coach Billy Bull by turning out in this ten-miler. Seventeen year-old Kev. insists that it was only a steady run.

1, G.Staines (Belgrave) 46:57; 2, L.Aguta (Kenya) 46:58; 3, F.Degefu (Ethipoia) 47:14; ... 97, K. Quinn 57:42

Report · page 50

Reebok Surrey CC League Division 1 Race 1 Brockwell Park, 19 Oct 1996: Matt Yates leads "The Revival"

The charge, with the Belgrave flag flying bravely in the breeze.

The start of another league campaign. We'd finished strongly at the end of the previous season with a 4th team place and as our President and Team Manager arrived early to hoist the new Belgrave flag aloft, hopes were high for a continuance of the upward trend.

"What's this ... a revival ?", called out South London Team Manager Mick Firth as the club colours proudly flew over Herne Hill territory in the stiff breeze. We could only hope so, for traditionally we seem to start off with an abysmal performance. But a revival it was. Before too long around thirty Belgrave runners and a dozen supporters had gathered at the foot of the mast and with Stu' Paton hurling good natured abuse at all and sundry our team gathered themselves for the strength-sapping uphill start.

From the very beginning Matt Yates was in the thick of the action as AFD's Jackson forced the pace up the slope with Marriott (HW), Major (SLH) and an unknown Thames man also in close attendance. Not so far back, in his first scoring run for the Bels., Steve Hewes had made a blistering start that he was shortly to pay for.

Going into the second lap the Thames man, now identified as 2nd-claimer Larry Matthews from Salford, made his break and forced open what began to look like a winning lead. Matt was going well in the chasing bunch with Roger Alsop and Jim Estall also claiming forward positions. But the revelation for the Bels. was the battling performances from our middle order men. Grouped together in two's and three's it was by no means certain who would fill out the scoring ten.

It was far from over up front as well. The nearer the leader got to the finish, the more contorted became his features as the others slowly reeled him in. Climbing the last hill it looked as if Matt might just have the Midlander in range as he left Simpson and Major behind in a last ditch assault but once the hair-pin turn at the top had been negotiated and they dropped back down the slope to the finish we could see that the Belgrave man was going to have to settle for the runner-up's slot.

Borough of Hounslow had the team race sewn up with six men in the first dozen but we were looking as good as anyone else at this stage as tremendous running from Roger gave him a final position of 13th with Jim first veteran home ahead of Herne Hill's Bob Payne by three places and twelve seconds. Way above expectations Bruce Barton and the Daves Beaver and Pamah came home in the 40s with Charlie Dickinson and Mike Webb also entering the funnel together in the 50's. Said Chettati and a shattered Steve Hewes closed in the team but with Mike Kazimierski and Phil Carstairs close behind we had plenty of back-up.

Our last scorer was home ahead of his Thames counterpart but the Roehampton based club had too many men in the 'teens and thirties so it was 2nd for them and a highly pleasing 3rd for the Bels. Our best opening match since 1986 and before that 1973!

The Belgrave youngsters were very thin on the ground and had to take last scoring positions to fill out the score sheet. But what they lacked in numbers they made up for in individual prowess as Amir celebrated his return to England with 2nd in the Under 17 race, Steve Beaver made an early bid to retain his overall Under 15 trophy and Denny DeEmmony opened a few eyes with his powerful running to win the youngest category by a full 25 seconds.

156 finished

Report · page 51

Chicago Marathon, 20 Oct 1996: Evans races to 3rd fastest ever British Marathon time

Chicago - home of some world famous sporting outfits. The White Sox baseball team come from this part of the world as do the Bears Football team but on this occasion the “windy city” was the setting for one of the best ever British marathon victories. And while Paul Evans charged to the third fastest ever time by a Briton, Gary Staines took another minute off his best to place 5th and come home in 2:11:25 in only his third run out over the distance. What a pair.

Gary had been aiming for the event for some time. Knee and back niggles had hampered his preparations but winning the Great South 10 miler from the outstanding African Lameck Aguta put him into the frame of mind where he felt he had a 2:10-2:11 in him and how right he was. Learning more about the race all the time, it surely must be on the cards that “Stainesy” also has a 2:08 to come when everything goes right.

For Paul an outing in Chicago only came together in the last few weeks before the event. Originally he was pencilled in for the New York race a little later but amazingly the organisers of that event couldn't find room in their budget for him. New York's loss was Chicago's gain. Like Gary he went into the race with a super 10 miler behind him, a 46:36 clocking in Amsterdam's Dam to Dam race in September.

The early pace was strong and both Belgravians felt uncomfortable early on. Half-way was reached in 64:12 but in the next few miles the pace dropped a little to just outside 5 minute miles. Paul was now feeling great, however, and his only worry was whether he should put in a burst to break things up a bit. Even this concern was removed when the Russian Svetsov took up the initiative and did the work for him. Basildon's Eamonn Martin and Gary Staines dropped away as only the American Lawson joined Evans in clinging on to the Russian's tail. A 25 metre gap opened at one point but at 18 miles three consecutive sub-4:50s put Evans in the clear. By 23 miles he was a minute up and enjoying the sensation of cruising to his first ever marathon win in eleven attempts. Gary was still lying 4th with only a quarter mile to go but a typical Eamonn Martin late charge shunted him back a place. With second-claimer Tony Barden in 26th it was a great day for the Bels.

1, P.Evans (Bel) 2:08:52; 2, L.Lawson (USA) 2:10:04; 3, L.Svetsov (Russia) 2:10:33; 5, G.Staines 2:11:25; 26, T.Barden 2:26:34.

Paul EvansGary StainesTony BardenLameck AgutaSvetsovEamonn MartinLawson

Report · page 52

South of Thames “Junior” Champs. Pyecroft, Nr. Aldershot, 9 Nov 96: Roger and out !

Banned at last! A first place for Roger Alsop means that we will have to look elsewhere for the cornerstone of our South of Thames “Junior” team for 1997. When interviewed by AW's Martin Duff when the race was over and asked “When did you think you could win it?” Roger, not normally known for hiding his light under a bushell, had to bite his lip to stop himself from replying, “As soon as I got out of the car!” Frankly though, that's how confident he felt going into the race.

What a shame that we were unable to supply our cross-country Vice-Captain with a full team to back him up and gain a set of team medals. Said Chettati was up 16 places on last year and Kevin Quinn ran to a superb 20th in his first outing in this event. We only needed another runner in the top forty to win team silver while anyone in the top 70 would have given us bronze. To be fair, we had runners at home who had offered to turn out if necessary to complete a team but with the expectation of there being some six Belgravians on the start line without them, and being in the middle of a busy period of races week in and week out, it was felt that there should be no arm-twisting.

In clear and bright conditions our man was quickly on the shoulder of the leader as the pack left the DERA sports ground and headed off for two laps of forest paths. Mick Wood's Aldershot boys were all to the fore on their home territory and they hassled Roger with their six men in the first ten: a gentle push here and there had to be administered by the Belgravian as they chopped and changed their pace around him. By the end of the first lap two men had gone clear, AFD's schoolboy champion Alan Barnes with Roger sticking close to him. The younger man just had to be in front and when Roger occasionally drifted alongside to pressure him, Barnes played right into the Belgravian's hands by trying to forge away again when in actual fact he was already at nearly full throttle with the needle swinging over towards ‘empty’.

Two-thirds of the way into the race and it was time to go. Still running within himself Roger stepped up a gear and although Barnes could be heard breathing heavily just behind for a while, when the hills were reached, all became silent as the claret and gold vested figure went into an ever-growing lead. Only a particularly deep and flooded pothole gave him any trouble from here on and Roger's lead was over 20 seconds as he swung back onto the playing field for the finish.

Both Kevin and Said enjoyed themselves as they worked their way strongly through the field and with Roger now barred from the competition they will form the nucleus of next year's team.

154 finished. 23 teams closed in.

All smiles as Roger Alsop crosses the finish line at Pyecroft with the second man not yet in sight.

Report · page 53

Reebok Surrey CC League Division 1 Race 2 Kingston Vale, 16 Nov 1996: Mixed fortunes on the Common

Having gone to an unexpected 3rd team place in race one and with the team, if anything, promising to be a little stronger this time out, we could be forgiven for starting to look for a 2nd spot behind the strong Hounslow outfit. Matt Yates had completed his week's training by lunchtime but still turned out to spearhead the team and Graham Adams was back in the squad after sitting out the opening match with a calf injury. Yep, surely we could sneak a 2nd on what was more or less our home patch ... but the race was to be full of surprises and as the team declaration was handed in we were missing two of our key men in Dave Pamah and Dave Beaver although boosted by the very welcome return to the fold of a gaunt and fit looking Ray Foley.

Again we had a large contingent on the line and as the starter called, “Ready,” our Captain showed his race intentions by streaking off and taking the field with him; a belated, “Go,” accompanied the tail-enders over the line. Guest runner and Sunday morning visitor to Belgrave Hall, Richard Nerurkar had looked ominously confident as he had loosened up before the start, declining with a smile the offer of a claret and gold vest to race in. As befits a triple-National winner he was immediately at the head of the field that had Aldershot's Garner and Jackson, Herne Hill's Jon Solly, our own Matt Yates and Croydon's McCallum all vying for forward positions. But climbing the hill to the top of the course only a mile out, Nerurkar decided that it was not going to be a social event and by the mid-race point a mile and a half later had gone 50 seconds clear.

Further drama was unfolding back down the field. Graham Adams had quite uncharacteristically completely “blown up” after a mile and for him the rest of the race became purgatory. Another to suffer miserably when he thought he had fully recovered from a cold was Donal O'Riordan, so at half-way, two of our expected scorers were right out of it. Roger Alsop and Jim Estall had both had hard races the previous week but were comfortably up around the 20 mark but the gap between them and the majority of the Belgrave team was obviously going to be too much for that 2nd team place.

At the finish positions were much the same. Bruce Barton had fallen heavily but gamely ran on well to lead home the engine-room men three places up on his Brockwell Park outing - and this in a higher standard field. Ray Foley was next in - great to have him back - just ahead of Steve Hewes who this time listened to wise old harrier Charlie Dickinson and worked his way through instead of blasting it from the start. Our 7th and 8th men, Said Chettati and Mike Kazimierski, also improved. Sadly we couldn't count Demetrius Onoufrio, our 2nd-claimer from Southampton Runners who had not been registered, so it was left to Phil Carstairs (still with the New York marathon in his legs) and Mark Anderson to close in the team.

A quick tally up showed that we were 5th. It was a little disappointing at first but a measure of what we are now expecting of ourselves; and looking back we had done pretty well, getting within 20 points of our opening score in spite of problems. Aldershot had surprised while Borough of Hounslow slipped back a little, their 10th man coming home behind ours but with five men between 23 and 27 crossing the finish in line abreast formation, they kept their overall score down. The Boro' men seem unlikely now to lose their league lead but only 177 points covers the next five clubs so there is plenty left to run for.

Unfortunately, while the seniors seem to have an enthusiastic and swelling pool of runners to call upon, our youngsters' numbers have dwindled. Amir Becirodzic was second man home again, chasing a different Aldershot lad this time, and Denny DeEmmony was first Belgrave Under 13 across the finish line in 4th, first three all being Aldershot boys.

153 finished

Report · page 54

Surrey Cross Country Champs. Happy Valley, Old Coulsdon, 14 Dec 1996: Thirty barren seasons come to an end

The boys were geared up for a set of medals. There was no way they were going home without something on this day. Roger Alsop's winter had been tailored towards a forward position in this event but the onset of a cold during the week meant that he was now only turning out for the team's sake. Jim Estall was taking a well-earned break after successive week's of road and country battles - but still the mood was one of determination. And then when Gary Staines showed up for his first race since Chicago, putting himself up as a target for Solly and Croydon's young McCallum to have a pop at, a quiet ripple of excitement went through the team. Maybe we could nail Boxhill!

When the 200 plus runners surged across the frosty field and down into Happy Valley, Belgrave vests were already prominent in the top thirty with Jon Solly leading the charge. (Little known fact: Jon was actually born in Denmark Road Wimbledon.) SLH's Stuart Major, a favourite before the big boys showed up, took a turn at the head of the field after a mile or so, with first year Senior McCallum, Staines, Solly and Boxhill's Haywood in full cry behind. The Belgrave team was holding up well with Dave Beaver our second man in the late ‘teens and the other lads queueing up behind - except Ray Foley who was surprisingly lying about 80th. It looked as if he would be the Belgrave man to miss out on a medal.

Halfway. Now Solly was making his bid; Mr. Staines was not going to find this an easy race by any means. A five metre gap was opened up and before long it had doubled, the Herne Hill Harrier racing away on the downhill and flatter stretches with Gary painfully recovering a metre or two with every climb. Dave Beaver now had Roger and Graham Adams in close attendance and Bruce Barton followed by Said Chettati were also moving well not too much further behind. Simon Fairbrother had eased his way through to join club-mate Haywood and with another man in 11th the Boxhill Billies were having another strong team run - but, fingers crossed, had to go back too far for their 5th and 6th men. Amazingly Ray Foley was now into the 40's; he'd been picking off runners like cherries from a tree - and his basket was still far from full.

One more lap of Happy Valley to go and Solly's lead had, if anything, grown a little more. True there was a slight hesitation as he asked a pointsman if it was time yet to turn off up the long climb to the finish but his raking stride was relentless down the slope into the last circuit and Gary later confessed to severe doubts as to whether it was all over. Teamwise we looked better than ever. Said was suffering, slowly falling towards the back of the 30s, but Ray was running like a dream; with arms and legs pumping he was flying past man after man, seemingly feeling no pain. With five men now between 17 and 28, and Gary up ahead, surely the race was ours. But the fat lady was in no mood to sing just yet.

Your correspondent and other supporters scurried to the top of the course and awaited the arrival of the runners. Frenzied shouting from spectators who could see down the slope told us that it was far from being all over and as the runners burst onto the finish field it was Gary's distinctive figure leaning into the climb a few paces ahead of the Herne Hill man. He'd used every ounce of his strength on that final incline and in the last minutes Jon Solly had crumbled. What a race. Everyone seemed to have run themselves into the ground. McCallum came home a minute later, eventually getting the better of Major and then it was three Boxhill men in quick succession before the finishers went into double figures.

Steaming up the straight and still taking places to the line came our second man Ray Foley - a super run - and another tremendous performance came from Dave Beaver who just had the edge over Roger, but where was ‘Gerry’ Adams? Bruce finished strongly, delighted with his effort and still no ‘Gerry’. Five Belgrave in and then five Boxhill. Said painfully made his way to the funnel, grittily keeping ahead of Boxhill's last man and it was time for the maths. It seemed we'd done it by one point!

A disconsolate ‘Gerry’ limped back but out of the race. He'd badly turned an ankle in the woods only a mile from the finish and now had to face the fact that as Captain he was going to be the man who didn't get a cherished ‘Surrey’ gold medal.

When the non-scorers were taken out our winning margin became four points and after the presentation Boxhill's Robin Batchelor ruefully had to hand over the Cup. Wrapped in a plastic bag and lying with their kit-bags the trophy hadn't been handed in to the officials before the start - perhaps they'd not expected to lose it!

Belgrave activity had been a bit sparse in the rest of the day's events. Nikki Haines ran to a fine 5th place in the Senior Women's 4 miles and it was good to see Morag Andrews and Jacinta Moore back out - but no team. No team either in the Under 20 Men's race. Adam Jones had been racing well in college events and was looking to go out of the age group in style. An untimely bout of ‘flu’ in the days before put paid to his plans, however, and he was forced to withdraw. This left us with Kevin Quinn and Simon Alexander to run as individuals - both finishing above the winning team's first scorer. From a large Under 15 Boys' field, Stephen Beaver's 7th proved that regular steady training is paying off and he may well have earned himself a trip to the Inter-Counties.

A cold-ridden Roger Alsop (8) was unable to hold back the ever improving Dave Beaver (12) at Happy Valley

Feature · page 54

It's coming home ...

“ ... it's coming home ... ”. We're pretty sure the soccer fans among you won't mind us borrowing the opening lines from the Euro '96 anthem - but frankly, the phrase just about sums up what winning this event means to Belgrave Harriers. Whatever success we gain in Track & Field and however you measure the prowess of an athletic club, we know that underlying it all we started as a Harrier club - and it hurts when we are no longer seen as one. When Surrey County's magnificent F.N.Pickett Cup was displayed at Committee two days after our Surrey team win, the look on Bill Lucas' face was an absolute treat to behold; and wouldn't old Tom have been delighted. As you will see from the list of previous winners, we have to go back 30 years for our previous victory when our outstanding runner of that day was Gerry North. In 1967 we went on to place 2nd in the ‘Southern’ and 4th in the ‘National’, winning the Surrey League on the way - so let's not get too excited, we don't expect that just yet! There's also a lot of difference between winning it once off and maintaining a vice-like grip on it for a few years. But nevertheless, we can feel very pleased with our progress and the fact that our runners are beginning to move in the right direction, together. Good on you boys and well done Gary Staines for spearheading the team!

Bill LucasTomGerry NorthGary Staines

Report · page 55

Reebok Surrey CC League Division 1 Race 3 Lloyd Park, Croydon, 4 Jan 97: The “Engine-room” packs to 2nd

Lloyd Park manages to throw up a variety of conditions over the years and this time it took on the disguise of an Arctic waste. With the ground already frozen to a depth of several inches, the still conditions had brought down a hard frost which had bushes, trees and grassland alike covered in a white mantle and with daylight already seeming to fail as race-time approached and with a hint of snow in the air - was it cold!

It was a day only for the hardy and as the teams were registered it was apparent that some clubs, what with ‘flu’ and mid-winter apathy, were struggling to declare ten names. That put the Team Manager in jovial mood for he knew that he already had ten men jogging round the course to check out the icy spots. Gary Staines was suffering from a cold but had turned up to watch and gee up the lads, as had Stuart Paton, so with supporters on hand as well, the team was in good spirits and looking for another improvement in our fortunes.

Spirits were lifted higher still when James Venning stormed to a convincing victory in the under 17 race. A tough looking runner this one and we'll be keeping an eye on his progress over the next few years. James just had time to give further reports on the course before the Seniors were lined up ... and away.

A good circuit for spectators this one but with only part of the first lap gone we were startled to find that no Bels. featured in the top twenty while Hounslow and Aldershot seemed to be packing them in. Dave Beaver, clad only in singlet and shorts while many of the runners were sporting tights if not track-suits and woolly hats, was working his way through nicely as was Jim Estall but positions were much the same as the field set out on the second circuit.

Climbing the steep, slippery slope before heading out on the last long loop we still only had Dave and Jim inside the first score but then the Bels came flooding through too fast for numbed fingers to keep a team tally. It was going to be interesting. England Team Manager Dave Clarke was using his experience and making a determined run through the field for Hercules Wimbledon but Hounslow's Simpson was dominating the race with McCallum, on his own ground, also looking comfortable.

All around that last lap our team continued to consolidate but even en masse they were unable to break into the top dozen or so runners. But now it was time to dash over to the finish and check off the runners as they came home.

The leading two had maintained their positions but Clarke had put on a real charge to get up to 3rd. Already providing the winner, Hounslow then had Usher in 4th and another man in 10th but apart from them the top ten finishers were evenly distributed between every other club - except the Bels. Fifteen men home and still no claret and gold ... and then, with Dave Beaver leading the way, it was all Belgrave. Eight men home in 40 and then, just over two minutes after our first man had entered the funnel, the team was closed in by Junior Galley and Mike Webb. Only Aldershot had got their complete team home ahead of us while Hounslow, Boxhill and Thames Hare & Hounds waited ... and waited.

What a close race and what a great score for us. With second team place on the day and moving into 3rd overall we confirmed something that we have known all along but have hardly dared speak of: we can win these races.

James Venning, seen here in middle-distance action in the McDonalds YA League during the summer, has carried his form over nicely to the winter. At Lloyd Park he ran away from the pack in the Under 17 Race to set the scene nicely for our Seniors.

Feature · page 56

The Christmas Day Paarlauf

Based on an unfinished article penned by Steve Williams c1986 and completed by Alan Mead with great assistance from Pete Mulholland (Hercules Wimbledon AC) and Dave Beard (General Secretary of Lauriston Runners Club).

“FRIENDLINESS was, as befitted the season, the keynote of a highly successful Paarlauf, a newly instituted Christmas feature, run around the green at Wimbledon Common on Christmas morning.” That was how The Belgravian introduced its report on the inaugural half hour two man relay held on that most familiar of territory in 1954. Then, in fine and warm conditions, but with a wind from the north-west, 12 runners turned out to gain their first experience of what was described as a comparatively new type of relay.

The race owes its origin to Charlie Smart, the Belgrave international distance runner who served with the Control Commission and competed in Germany after the war. He brought back the Continental idea of two man teams competing in an event known as the Paarlauf, or paired run, and Arthur Whitehead, owner of Lauriston Cottage on the edge of Wimbledon Common and always a fan of Charlie's, took up the concept and helped bring it to fruition.

To ensure reasonably even pairings, the names of the six best runners were placed in one hat and the remainder in another; this method turning out to be, “quite satisfactory as the field kept surprisingly close together throughout the race, 332 yards covering them all at the finish.” This was in the days before Arthur Whitehead had opened Lauriston Cottage to all athletes and only Belgravians took part in the first race. However, it was the late Aubrey Reeve, the Hon. Secretary of Wimbledon AC who made the draw and the race was started outside the cottage by John Jewell, the Assistant Hon. Secretary of the Road Runners Club. Of course Arthur Whitehead and Charlie Smart also officiated. The 1,415 yards circuit was conveniently divided into two legs by the short recovery across the green adjacent to Rushmere Pond to Cannizaro Road and vice versa.

The format established

The inaugural winners were Len Bishop and Eric Gebbett with 6 miles 145 yards, a modest distance as later events were to show, caused perhaps by inexperience but certainly affected by the whistle being blown at 27 minutes, after which no further change was allowed, instead of the now used 28 minutes. One of the first to suffer the long last leg was Gordon Biscoe as his ‘senior’ partner Charlie Walker, reported as being caught napping, left Gordon in possession of the baton. After the race everyone adjourned to the cottage to wash down and partake of an enormous baking of mince pies with a hot and rather alcoholic witches brew concocted by Arthur Whitehead. And as the format for this popular event was established, it has remained virtually unchanged ever since.

The following year the event took place on Christmas Eve and the famous exploding dustbin was born when, as The Belgravian reported, “ ... dispensing with the usual method of starting, Arthur Whitehead and Arthur Penstone had rigged up a small explosive charge inside a dustbin and the explosion which started the race brought back memories of the war when the Common was an exercise ground for the Army.” In later days, the dustbin was only used to sound time for no further changeover and then for the 30 minute finish. A more effective way of ensuring that everyone heard was difficult to imagine at the time although the Christmas Day explosions were brought to an end years later when a Wimbledon Common Ranger objected due to the noise frightening the horses. Today's solution, when explosions of any kind are frowned upon, is an easily carried klaxon driven by a canister of compressed air.

Word of this interesting event quickly spread and in 1956 three Hercules AC pairings and one from Herne Hill Harriers joined eight from Belgrave to contest the event which was retained for the Bels by the two Charlies - Walker and Henn - in a more respectable 6 miles 656 yards. But it was inevitable that as Belgrave continued to draw for partners, in the spirit of the festivity, that we would soon lose a hold on the race to a nominated pairing from another club. The following year, a Ranelagh pair of Bill Bird (now Secretary of the Reebok Surrey Cross Country League) and Brian Nott (to become a second-claim Belgravian in the years ahead) set a new record of 6 miles 947 yards.

In 1962, The Belgravian records that 18 pairs took part with 12 of these from visiting clubs. “This was the 9th year of the race and its popularity grows on each occasion. The winning pair, Gowan and Derham, were the only ones to complete 8 circuits for a new record of 6 miles 1,094 yards.” The first Bels pairing was 8th and it was commented upon that “the time has probably come for our stars to partner each other.” A Belgrave record of 6 miles 697 yards was set by Pat Newell and David Baines.

Lauriston Runners

On 30th October 1966 a meeting was held in the changing rooms at Lauriston Cottage at which it was agreed that those present would form a new club, Lauriston Runners. With the informal group of runners using the cottage as a training base now banded together as an open club, it was natural that the race should become organised under their auspices and at the same time the need for the Bels to change their ‘A’ and ‘B’ formula disappeared as at that inaugural Lauriston Runners meeting it was proposed by Bob Holt of Hercules AC, now second-claim Lauriston as well, that pairings for the Paarlauf should be made from ‘A’ and ‘B’ competitors to mitigate the disparity in competition. This had reached a peak the previous year when Millard and Taylor of Cambridge Harriers set a record of 6 miles 1,406 yards (4mins 24secs. miling pace) to leave all the Belgrave pairings way behind. That distance naturally still stands as the record as it is a rare occurrence for a genuine ‘A’ and ‘B’ pairing to exceed 6 miles 1,000 yards. Bels first pair of Steer and Walker set a Belgrave best of 6 miles 879 yards in 11th place that year.

1966 therefore saw the start of what might be described as the general hubbub and banter that is thrown up when the pairings are drawn - “What's ‘e doing in the ‘B’ hat!?” - as much a highlight of the event as the race itself! For Graham Taylor the rule change made no difference. Then at the top of his form, in a year which had seen him clock a sub-1hr. 40min. Belgrave ‘20’, it made no difference whether he ran with another ‘recognised’ runner or not. Drawn with ‘B’ runner Cheeseman of South London Harriers he was first again with 6 miles 1,067 yards.

Athletic folk-lore

The years drifted past and stories connected with the event have become part of local athletic folk-lore.

John Phelan (Hercules Wimbledon) ran his first Paarlauf in 1962 and he also ran in 1996 - a span of 34 years in which he has competed 20 times, twice as a winner. But John's 34 year love affair with the race was easily eclipsed when Belgrave's Alan Black returned to the race this winter after a lapse of 14 Christmas-tides. Alan's first Paarlauf was in 1955 - giving him a span of 41 years in which he has clocked up 24 runs - also with two wins.

Jocular accusations of “rigging” were made in 1978 when Alan, a one-time Scottish International, was classed as a ‘B’ runner. He found himself paired with “HW's” Dave Clarke, three times National Cross Country Champion and twice runner-up. It was the 25th running of the event and as the only man from the early races still turning out it did seem fitting that he should share in a “silver jubilee” victory. However, with 48 runners that year there were some other very good ‘B’ runners around including our own Pete Gilbey, then Surrey AC, and “HW's” Mike Kortenray and Pete Clarke. It was all above board.

One further note regarding Alan Black: in the latest running of the event when paired with Paul Warburton they achieved the distinction of being the first twosome ever not to exceed 5 miles!

As befits a National Champion Dave Clarke is the man who has the most victories to his name with five. Next comes Ealing & Southall's Pete Barratt whose three wins came in consecutive years, each time with a different Ranelagh partner, and Belgrave's John Gladwin, also on three.

Ken Ashby (Hercules Wimbledon) has won twice as a ‘B’ runner, partnering his club-mate John Sullivan in 1969 and Bob Richardson of Blackheath in 1974. Ken's were fitting victories as his birthday also falls on December 25th. The rumour that, like a former celebrant of this birthdate, he could walk on water and used this ability to take a short cut across Rushmere Pond between his stages has been refuted by those who were present.

The first woman to run was Angie Thomas who partnered her Thames Valley Harrier husband Clive in 1977 and there have been many other occasions of the race being used as an energetic Christmas morning family outing. In 1966 the Beard brothers turned out in force - Mike, Paul and Dave, all of Hercules AC (the Hercules and Wimbledon clubs amalgamated on 1st September 1967). And this feat was repeated by another threesome from “HW” in 1978 - Chris, Dave and Pete Clarke. There has only ever been one family victory and this way back in 1958 when the brothers Bird, Bill and 14 year old David took home the prize. Fathers and sons have taken part (eg. Belgrave's Keith and Mark Sinclair) and in 1995 there was a Belgrave father and son pairing when Dave and Steve Beaver took to the road.

A sensation occurred in 1987 when the team of South London's John Roberts and Belgrave's John Wasborough found themselves both running at the same time! It seems that “Wassy” thought Roberts would have been among the leaders at the first takeover and not seeing him among the early arrivals, thought he must have missed him so away he went. Meanwhile Roberts, who had made a steady start, arrived at the changeover to find no “Wassy” and decided to continue. A few minutes later Roberts found himself passing “Wassy” who was then told in no uncertain terms to carry on!

The Kenyans

The Kenyans arrived in 1990 and what a baptism they received. Conditions were atrocious as a cold westerly wind brought heavy rain and sleet throughout the race. The recording of the number of laps run and the final measuring up was impossible. Only the first five places could be guaranteed as being correct and approximate measurements only could be made for the first three home. Osoro Ondoro of Kenya shared his win with Crawley AC's A. Reader with a string of famous African names relegated to the main and unrecorded body of the field: John Ngugi, Richard Chelimo, William Mutwol, Susan Sirma, etc. In spite of the weather they all enjoyed themselves, especially the after race refreshments, and they surprised the “regulars” no end when they washed themselves down by making use of the ice-cold water hose. They didn't show up the following year but were back in ‘92 and ‘95. These multi-world record holders and stars of tracks around the world all pay their £1 race entrance fee, enjoying the banter and happy to share their runs and Christmas mornings with unknown club runners.

Surprisingly, there seems to have been a noticeable drop in standards over the years and this in spite of the appearance of the Kenyans. In 1965 no fewer than 26 of the 32 teams beat 6 miles, a figure that was equalled the following Christmas, the first year of the ‘draw’. In 1994 only one pair achieved that feat and only two in the latest running of the event. One must remember though, that in the mid ‘sixties Surrey boasted the finest distance runners in the country, a fact borne out by victory in the Inter-Counties Cross Country Championship. But as far as the Paarlauf is concerned no-one is the slightest bit bothered about the situation, it being generally agreed that the main point of the race is for the runners to enjoy themselves in a light-hearted Christmas event where despite “professionalism” the first three pairs only receive small token prizes. As Lauriston Runners' General Secretary Dave Beard says,

“The prize for all those involved is the enjoyment in taking part whether actively or helping out or merely spectating”.

With the race now well into its 5th decade, long may these sentiments prevail.

* The 1967 race ran for 31 minutes
