Feature · page 59

Jim Estall, Belgrave Harriers & England

CHARLIE DICKINSON

CHARLIE DICKINSON updates us on the 1996 season of one of Britain's top veteran runners.

Most runners are used to high and low points in their career, but few can have experienced such a turbulent season as Jim Estall in 1996. His worst London Marathon performance for many years in the heat of April was followed by weeks of illness and injury. But slowly, Jim picked up the pieces, started to run well again in the summer, and a series of fime Autumn performances saw him selected for the England Veterans Cross Country team for the Home International in Irvine where he won team gold and individual bronze medals.

Perhaps the Marathon disappointment, where he ran 2hr 35min maybe ten minutes slower than expected, was all the worse after such a promising build up throughtout the late winter and early spring. Added to his usual consistent cross country performances were a fine 68:15 half marathon, 1hr 50min in the Worthing ‘20’, and his best ever time in the South of England 12-stage at Thurrock after a mid-week 32 minute 10km in the Civil Service Championships.

However, two days before the London Marathon he started to notice a sore throat. He managed to run at 5 min. 30 sec. miles to Greenwich, but then began to slow, reaching half way in 74 minutes. By the end, feeling very unwell and dehydrated in the unusually high temperature, he dropped to 6 min. 15 sec. miles and eventually was just glad to finish. The first place team prize along with Paul Evans, Gary Staines and Junior Galley was at least some compensation for sticking it out but Jim was left ill and deflated for several weeks after the race.

Nor was his recovery helped when he injured his foot while doing some fencing work in the garden. In fact it took him even longer to get over the accident than the effects of the marathon and it wasn't until June that he felt fit enough to race again.

Answering a late call to run the 5000m for the British League team at Copthall, Jim surprised himself by running 15:37 off just a few weeks of steady running and no track work. He followed this with a 15:20 in the Southern league and a progression of excellent midweek VAC 5.2 mile runs in Battersea Park. In August he ran a solo 26:29, only a couple of seconds slower than Laurie O'Hara's course record. Jim was back with a vengeance. Two weeks later he ran away from the field in the VAC/Belgrave 10000m Championships, breaking the record in 31:31.6, a time which ranked him second M40 in the country.

He then began to think again about the Veterans International cross country to be held in Scotland in November. He had first thought about the possiblity of selection back in the previous Autumn after beating the then British veterans cross country champion Barry Atwell in the Surrey Championships in December 1995, and again in the Surrey league in the new year.

The Autumn saw a wonderful series of performances from Jim. 60th in the Great North run in 70:38, an 18:57 relay leg for the senior team at Aldershot in the South of England 6-Stage, a time which would have placed him second only to Mike Gratton if he had run in the Veterans race. Then on October 6th perhaps his finest ever race in the British veterans 10km road championships at Solihull.

Here Jim finished second to supervet Nigel Gates, beating all the rest of the best veterans in the country and running 31:31 on an undulating course. It was here that Jim earned his England vest.

So to Scotland for the Veterans International cross country on November 10th. On a beautiful sunny day on a rich green hilly four lap course overlooking the Isle of Arran Jim was determined to “give it a real go”. He did just that, chasing the in form Derek Mullin after the initial mad rush. At the end of the first lap Peter Yeomans had briefly taken the lead but by the time the field reached the awesome hill at the back of the course for the second time Mullin was beginning to pull away from Yeomans and Jim.

Jim Estall showed great determination over the next lap and a half, never giving up the chase, with the first two shorter striding and lighter runners perhaps more suited to the sharply undulating terrain. Eventually, Mike Hager the real tough guy in veteran athletics and leading the England M45 team, came past Jim and finally Yeomans to snatch second place. When it looked as if Jim could still just about catch Yeomans with a quarter of a mile to go he felt his calf muscles tie up and had to settle for fourth.

However, as Hager was an M45, this wonderful performance earned Jim the bronze medal and with the first four England M40's closing in 5th place, the result of the team was a foregone conclusion - gold. The weekend in Scotland was perhaps the highlight of Jim's running career and proof to all of us that we should never give up.

Above: British League 5,000 metre duty at Copthall Stadium.

Right: It's August, and Jim runs 26:29 for the VAC 5.2 mile race. The Battersea Park Peace Pagoda has only ever witnessed one faster run - and that by another Belgrave super-vet Laurie O'Hara.

Left: With the Club 10,000 metre Championship just 40 seconds old, Jim has already put his cards on the table
